For the gluon fragmentation channel, we observe that the SFP and integral contributions roughly cancel against the SGP contribution. Hence, NLO corrections from this channel almost drop out. The NLO contributions for the
qq\to q
channel in particular become larger in Scenario 1 compared to
those in Scenario 0, but still remain small. The same statement holds for the
qq\to q^{\prime}
channel.

We again use the results to compute the right-left asymmetry, see Fig.
14
(right). We find that the asymmetries in Scenarios 0 and 1 do not differ too much, and one can readily draw the same conclusions as in the previous subsection. Whether or not the precision of EIC measurements of
A_{RL}
will be good enough to
distinguish between both scenarios and possibly rule out one of them, remains to be seen. Our simplified approach for the scale variation again predicts only moderately sized uncertainty bands for Scenario 1, the smallest out of all three scenarios.

IV.1.3
Scenario 2

Scenario 2 is described in Appendix
E.3
, and the corresponding contour plots for the correlation functions
F
and
G
are shown in Figs.
25
,
26
. These plots display more complex “structures” of the correlation functions on their support in
x,x^{\prime}
.
The reason is that the Fourier coefficients in Eq. (
238
) parameterizing Scenario 2 have been inflated by a factor of three relative to those for
Scenario 1, Eq. (
237
). As a result, the correlation functions increase for
x\neq x^{\prime}
. It is interesting to note that a model calculation in Ref.
[
93
]
based on an overlap representation of the functions
F
and
G
in terms of light-cone wave functions supports such a behavior. In any case, we emphasize that all three scenarios agree with all known constraints.

Figure 16:
Same as Fig.
13
, but for Scenario 2.

Figure 17:
Same as Fig.
14
, but now showing all Scenarios 0, 1 and 2 in a combined plot for easier comparison.

In Fig.
16
we plot the numerator of the right-left asymmetry for Scenario 2 for all contributing partonic channels at NLO. As for the other scenarios,
qg\to q

dominates over all other NLO channels. In contrast to the previous scenarios, the NLO corrections drastically change the LO result. In particular, the hard-pole contribution to the
qg\to q
channel is completely reversed compared to Scenario 0. All other contributions, i.e., SGP, SFP and integral contributions, are larger as well. Note that the LO result (which is
sensitive only to
x=x^{\prime}
) is the same in all the plots in Figs.
13
,
15
,
16
. When comparing the results
for the second-most relevant channel, gluon fragmentation
qg\to g
, for Scenarios 0 and 2 we observe that because of non-trivial cancellation of SGP, SFP and integral contributions the NLO corrections flip sign. As before the other channels
qq\to q
and
qq\to q^{\prime}
remain irrelevant.

In Fig.
17
we turn again to the asymmetry. Comparing
A_{RL}
for all three Scenarios, we observe that the NLO effects are largest for Scenario 2 where the unknown support “away from the SGP diagonal” of the correlation functions
F
and
G
causes the asymmetry to even flip sign around
mid pseudorapidity. One may hope that experimental data, once available from the EIC, would be able to resolve such large differences,
and thus help to distinguish between different scenarios. That being said, our simple implementation of scale variation produces theoretical uncertainty bands that do not show a clean sign change anymore.

IV.2
Single-Inclusive Jet Production

In the following we use our analytical result (
81
) for the NLO spin-dependent cross section, along with the NLO result for the unpolarized cross section taken from Refs.
[
36
,
88
]
, to study the numerical NLO effects on the right-left asymmetry
A_{RL}
in jet production. As input for the quark-gluon-quark correlation functions
F
and
G
we use the same model Scenarios 0, 1, 2 as above. We provide exemplary numerical studies for two jet radii,
R_{1}=0.2
and
R_{2}=0.7
.

Figure 18:
Contributions by the various NLO channels plotted versus the jet’s pseudorapidity
\eta_{j}
at fixed transverse momentum
P_{j,T}=5\,\mathrm{GeV}
at the EIC. The curves are obtained for Scenario 0 for the non-perturbative correlation functions
F
and
G
, see (
236
). The channels are:
qg\to\mathrm{jet}(q)
(upper left),

qg\to\mathrm{jet}(g)
(upper right),
qq\to\mathrm{jet}(q)
(lower left). In the lower right we show the full NLO asymmetry
A_{RL}
. Results are shown for two jet radii,
R_{1}=0.2
and
R_{2}=0.7
.

IV.2.1
Scenario 0

Our numerical results for Scenario 0 are shown in Fig.
18
. As for single-inclusive pion production we show plots for the NLO contributions by the
various partonic channels. The dominant channels are those originating from quark-gluon-quark correlations (
x^{\prime}\geq 0
), where either a radiated quark or gluon generates a jet in the partonic cross section. We note that for Scenario 0 large soft-gluon pole contributions as well as large hard-pole contributions appear in the

qg\to\mathrm{jet}(q)
channel, along with negligible soft-fermion and integral contributions. However, a partial cancellation of SGP and HP contributions leads to rather small NLO corrections. Since HP contributions do not appear in the
qg\to\mathrm{jet}(g)
channel, the whole NLO correction for that channel is generated by the SGP contribution and consequently is larger. The
qq\to\mathrm{jet}(q)
channel remains irrelevant, while the
qq\to\mathrm{jet}(q^{\prime})
channel vanishes for jets.

The right-left asymmetry
A_{RL}
for single-inclusive jet production is shown in the lower-right plot of Fig.
18
as function of
the jet’s pseudo-rapidity. We obtain a similar behavior for this observable in Scenario 0 as for
\pi^{+}
-production: the sum of all NLO corrections to the numerator
is even a little larger than the corrections to the unpolarized cross section in the denominator of
A_{RL}
. Overall, however, the asymmetry in Scenario 0 does not turn out to be too sensitive to NLO corrections, in contrast to the individual numerator and denominator. The NLO results show only little dependence on the jet radius
R
. Moreover, they are remarkably similar to their
\pi^{+}
counterpart in the left panel of Fig.
14
.

One would regard the jet asymmetry as an even cleaner observable than the pion asymmetry. From a theoretical point of view,
the main reason is that jet production is independent of fragmentation. As discussed earlier, this is especially important in the polarized case since
no twist-3 fragmentation correlation functions
are present for jets. In this sense, the plots in Fig.
18
represent a full NLO prediction
for the right-left asymmetry, based on Scenario 0.
We also note that the event rate for jets in the backward pseudo-rapidity region (where the asymmetry is largest) seems to be larger roughly by a factor of 100 compared to that for pion production.

IV.2.2
Scenario 1

Figure 19:
Same as Fig.
18
, but for Scenario 1.

Next, we consider jet production in Scenario 1 (see (
237
)). Our numerical results for this scenario are shown in Fig.
19
. Comparing the curves with the corresponding ones for pion production in Fig.
15
we observe a similar behavior for all channels, except that the HP contribution of the
qg\to\mathrm{jet}(q)
channel and the SFP contribution in the
qg\to\mathrm{jet}(g)
channel are modified compared to Scenario 0. This leads to a sign change for the asymmetry, indicating that in Scenario 1 the NLO corrections affect the asymmetry in jets to a somewhat greater extent than for pions.

IV.2.3
Scenario 2

Figure 20:
Same as Fig.
18
, but for Scenario 2.

This effect becomes even more pronounced for Scenario 2, as can be seen from Fig.
20
. Again, it is the HP contribution of the
qg\to\mathrm{jet}(q)
channel as well as the SFP contribution in the
qg\to\mathrm{jet}(g)
channel that drastically change the behavior of the right-left asymmetry. As we can see from the lower-right plot in Fig.
20
the NLO asymmetry peaks somewhere in the backward pseudo-rapidity region between
-2<\eta_{j}<-1
with a value
slightly above
4\,\%
, but with different sign compared to the LO curve. This means that at NLO the right-left asymmetry for single-inclusive jet production shows high sensitivity to the precise
form of the “off-diagonal” support of the
qgq
correlation functions
F
and
G
. This again indicates that future EIC data for the right-left (or the corresponding single-spin) asymmetry might be in the position to confirm or rule out scenarios for these functions.
In order to better illustrate this interesting result, we plot all three NLO asymmetries for jet production within Scenarios 0,1,2
in Fig.
21
, again for both jet radii.

Figure 21:
Comparison of the NLO asymmetries
A_{RL}
computed for Scenarios 0,1,2. The asymmetries are again plotted vs. the jet’s pseudorapidity
\eta_{j}
for jet cone radii
R_{1}=0.2

(left)
and
R_{2}=0.7

(right)
.

V
Conclusions

We have performed a next-to-leading order pQCD calculation
of the transverse single-spin asymmetry
for single-inclusive high-
P_{T}
hadron or jet production
in polarized lepton-nucleon scattering,
\ell N^{\uparrow}\to h\,\mathrm{or\,jet}\,X
.
We have taken into account all contributions to the spin-dependent cross section that, in collinear
factorization, enter with twist-3 parton correlation functions in the incoming nucleon.
As far as jet production is concerned, our calculation thus constitutes the first
complete NLO calculation of a single-inclusive single-transverse spin observable.
For hadron production, our calculation will need to be extended to account also for
twist-3 fragmentation contributions at NLO.
We have explicitly demonstrated that collinear twist-3 factorization holds at the one-loop level for the
contributions associated with twist-3 nucleon matrix elements. This finding provides a great boost
for theoretical confidence in the collinear twist-3 approach.

Furthermore, we have presented estimates of the size of the NLO corrections. To this end, we have
adopted three models for the relevant quark-gluon-quark correlation functions
F(x,x^{\prime})
and
G(x,x^{\prime})

and numerically computed the spin-dependent cross section based on our analytical NLO results, as well
as the transverse nucleon single-spin asymmetry for typical kinematics relevant at the EIC.
We found that a large part of the NLO corrections originate from hard-pole configurations where the
correlation functions are probed on their “off-diagonal” support
x\neq x^{\prime}
, implying that
future EIC data may have the potential to shed light on this so far completely unexplored region.
That said, the fact that numerous separate contributions influence the size of the spin asymmetry may
make it ultimately difficult to unravel all the individual contributions experimentally. Perhaps new
jet physics techniques such as the concept of “jet charge”
[
94
]
may be of value here at the EIC.
Possible identification of quark- and gluon-induced jets could help further to study individual partonic channels.

Our work can and will be extended in various ways. First, as mentioned above, we have
not yet addressed the twist-3 multiparton fragmentation contribution to the hadron production cross section.
We plan to work out the NLO corrections for these contributions in the future. We also note
that so far we have only properly included the evolution of the twist-2 functions in our phenomenological studies. For the twist-3 distribution
functions, on the other hand, we merely used the scale dependence of the
f_{1}^{q}
PDF, which is the only input of our model. The correct implementation of the true twist-3 scale evolution will be an important next step, e.g. for
studying the transverse-momentum dependence of the spin asymmetry, which may
offer additional insights into
F(x,x^{\prime})
and
G(x,x^{\prime})
. Of course,
for a fully consistent next-to-leading order study, evolution at next-to-leading order will
be required, which currently is not on the horizon.

A salient result of our paper is the sheer complexity of the calculation, even for the
relatively simple single-inclusive hadron or jet observables in lepton-nucleon scattering that we
have considered. In a way, our calculation serves as a “proof of principle” that full
NLO twist-3 calculations for transverse spin asymmetries are possible.
We expect that the methods presented in this paper will also be relevant for other, even more complicated processes,
especially for
p^{\uparrow}p\to hX
. A long-term goal would be to achieve a global QCD analysis at the NLO level
combining all possible data sets for transverse-spin effects from medium-energy fixed-target experiments all the way to RHIC and the EIC.
The results of this paper are one step towards the completion of this long-term goal.

Acknowledgements.
W.V. is grateful to Patriz Hinderer and Yuji Koike for their collaboration at initial stages of this work. We thank Jianwei Qiu for helpful discussions. This work has been supported by Deutsche Forschungsgemeinschaft (DFG) through the Research Unit FOR 2926 (Project No. 409651613).

Appendix A
Definition of (Multiparton) Correlation Functions in the Nucleon and Fragmentation Functions

In this appendix, we briefly recall the definitions of the multiparton correlation functions and fragmentation functions that are relevant for our work. These definitions and their properties have been discussed in Refs.
[
43
,
70
,
71
]
, and we refer the reader to these references for more details.

A.1
Quark-Quark Correlator

The first relevant field theoretical definition we provide is for the bare, unrenormalized quark-quark correlator,

\displaystyle\Phi_{\partial,ij}^{q,\rho}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{2\pi}\,\mathrm{e}^{i\lambda x}\langle P,S|\,\bar{q}_{j}(0)\,[0,\infty n]\,\times

(90)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\lim_{z_{T}\to 0}i\partial_{z_{T}}^{\rho}\left([\infty n+z_{T},\lambda n+z_{T}]\,q_{i}(\lambda n+z_{T})\right)\,|P,S\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\,M\,\epsilon^{Pn\rho S}\,\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to7.80904pt{\hfil/\hfil}\hss}P_{ij}\,f_{1T}^{\perp(1),q}(x)+...\,.

The diagonal matrix element between nucleon states of given momentum
P
and spin
S
in (
90
) contains the Heisenberg quark fields
q
(of flavor
q=u,d,...
) and Wilson lines
[\lambda n,\xi n]
from light-cone position
\xi
to
\lambda
along an additional light-like four-vector
n^{\mu}
(see Ref.
[
43
]
). This vector, unlike the nucleon momentum
P
, is unphysical and can be arbitrarily chosen as long as
n^{2}=0
and
P\cdot n=1
are satisfied. However,
n
is actually needed to characterize what we mean by the term
transverse
. In fact, we may introduce a
transverse
projector,

g_{T}^{\mu\nu}\equiv g^{\mu\nu}-P^{\mu}n^{\nu}-P^{\nu}n^{\mu}\,,\,\mathrm{with}\quad a_{T}^{\mu}\equiv g_{T}^{\mu\nu}a_{\nu}\,,

(91)

that separates out the transverse components
a_{T}^{\mu}
of an arbitrary four-vector
a^{\mu}
. So, if we say that the nucleon is transversely polarized, we mean
S^{\mu}=S_{T}^{\mu}
, or
n\cdot S=0
.

In the parameterization (
90
) we encounter the collinear function
f_{1T}^{\perp(1),q}(x)
which is commonly referred to as the
first moment of the Sivers function
(see Refs.
[
95
,
96
]
). It is accompanied by a factor
\epsilon^{\mu\nu\rho\sigma}P_{\mu}n_{\nu}S_{\sigma}\equiv\epsilon^{Pn\rho S}
, where
\epsilon
is the totally antisymmetric tensor with
\epsilon^{0123}=+1
. The ellipsis
...
in (
90
) indicates that there are several more terms that appear in the parameterization of the quark-quark correlator
\Phi_{\partial}^{q}
. However, these terms are irrelevant for the purpose of this paper.

The quark-quark correlator
\Phi_{\partial}
in (
90
) generates contributions to a transverse nucleon spin-dependent observable where
\Phi_{\partial}
is accompanied by partonic cross sections
\hat{\sigma}_{\partial}
in the collinear twist-3 formalism. These partonic cross sections
\hat{\sigma}_{\partial}
are calculated so that a kinematical approximation is applied to the four-momentum
k^{\mu}
of the initial state quarks (cf. (
16
)),

k^{\mu}\simeq x\,P^{\mu}-\tfrac{k_{T}^{2}}{2x}n^{\mu}+k_{T}^{\mu}\,,

with a subsequent Taylor expansion of the partonic cross section in the quark’s transverse momentum
k_{T}
. It is the first-order coefficient
\mathcal{O}(k_{T}^{1})
in this expansion that then constitutes the final collinear partonic cross section
\hat{\sigma}_{\partial}(k=x\,P)
. Since
\hat{\sigma}_{\partial}(k=x\,P)
is an indirect result of a non-zero transverse parton momentum, the contributions generated by
\Phi_{\partial}
in the collinear twist-3 formalism were called
kinematical
twist-3 contributions in Ref.
[
43
]
.

A.2
Gluon-Gluon Correlator

Similarly to the quark-quark correlator one can define the following analog for gluons (see Ref.
[
70
]
, note that this reference uses a different sign convention for the
\epsilon
-tensor):

\displaystyle\Phi_{\partial}^{g,\sigma\tau\rho}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{2\pi}e^{i\lambda x}\langle P,S|G^{n\sigma}(0)\,[0,\infty n]\,\times

(92)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\lim_{z_{T}\to 0}i\partial_{z_{T}}^{\rho}\left([\infty n+z_{T},\lambda n+z_{T}]\,G^{n\tau}(\lambda n+z_{T})\right)\,|P,S\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{M}{2}g_{T}^{\sigma\tau}\epsilon^{PnS\rho}G_{T}^{(1)}(x)\,-\,\frac{M}{8}\left(\epsilon^{PnS\{\sigma}g_{T}^{\tau\}\rho}\,+\,\epsilon^{Pn\rho\{\sigma}S_{T}^{\tau\}}\right)\Delta H_{T}^{(1)}(x)\,+\,\dots,

where
G^{n\sigma}\,\equiv\,n_{\mu}G^{\mu\sigma}
and
G^{n\tau}
are gluonic field strength tensors and an implicit sum over their color indices of the adjoint representation is understood. Again, the ellipsis
...
in (
92
) indicates terms in the parameterization that do not contribute to our calculation. Like the collinear function
f_{1T}^{\perp(1),q}(x)
from (
90
), the kinematical distributions
G_{T}^{(1)}(x)
and
\Delta H_{T}^{(1)}(x)
in (
92
) are the first moments of the corresponding transverse momentum dependent distributions (TMDs). For the same reason as for the quark-quark correlator, the contribution to an observable generated by the gluon-gluon correlator is counted as a
kinematical
twist-3 contribution.

A.3
Quark-Gluon-Quark Correlator

Another non-perturbative matrix element that is of relevance for this work is the
quark-gluon-quark correlator
,

\displaystyle\Phi_{F,ij}^{q,\rho}(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}\mu}{2\pi}\,\mathrm{e}^{i\lambda x^{\prime}}\mathrm{e}^{i\mu(x-x^{\prime})}\,\times

(93)

\displaystyle\hskip 56.9055pt\langle P,S|\,\bar{q}_{j}(0)\,[0,\mu n]\,ig\,G^{n\rho}(\mu n)\,[\mu n,\lambda n]\,q_{i}(\lambda n)\,|P,S\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}M\,i\epsilon^{Pn\rho S}\,\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to7.80904pt{\hfil/\hfil}\hss}P_{ij}\,F_{FT}^{q}(x,x^{\prime})-\frac{1}{2}M\,S_{T}^{\rho}\,(\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to7.80904pt{\hfil/\hfil}\hss}P\gamma_{5})_{ij}\,G_{FT}^{q}(x,x^{\prime})+...\,.

The matrix element in (
93
) contains, in addition to two quark fields
q
, the gluonic field strength tensor
G^{n\rho}\equiv n_{\mu}G^{\mu\rho}
. Because of the appearance of this third dynamical quantum field in (
93
) contributions to spin observables generated by
\Phi_{F}
are called
dynamical
twist-3 contributions (see Ref.
[
43
]
). The
quark-gluon-quark
correlation functions
F_{FT}^{q}
and
G_{FT}^{q}
, as introduced, for example, in Ref.
[
43
]
, are key to this paper whereas other structures in the second line of Eq. (
93
), denoted by
...
, are irrelevant for our purposes. In order to ease the notation, from now on and throughout this paper we shall drop the subscript
FT
for the quark-gluon-quark correlation functions and simply denote them as
F,G
instead of
F_{FT},G_{FT}
.

The
quark-gluon-quark
correlation functions
F
and
G

depend on two light-cone momentum fractions
x
and
x^{\prime}
. Their support
is constrained by the conditions
-1\leq x,x^{\prime}\leq 1
and
|x-x^{\prime}|\leq 1
. Most importantly, the vector-type function
F
is symmetric under exchange
x\leftrightarrow x^{\prime}
, while the axial vector-type function
G
is antisymmetric:

\displaystyle F(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+F(x^{\prime},x)\,,

\displaystyle G(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-G(x^{\prime},x)\,.

(94)

Consequently,
G(x,x)=0
. The functions
F
,
G
also contain information on their corresponding antiquark content in the region of negative
x
and
x^{\prime}
, that is,
F^{\bar{q}}(x,x^{\prime})=F^{q}(-x,-x^{\prime})
and
G^{\bar{q}}(x,x^{\prime})=G^{q}(-x,-x^{\prime})

[
43
]
.

At higher orders, not only the quark-gluon-quark correlation functions but also their derivatives become important. To be precise, in the following we consider the two functions
F(x_{1},x_{2})
and
G(x_{1},x_{2})
as generic functions of two variables
x_{1}
,
x_{2}
. We perform the partial derivatives with respect to
x_{1}
or
x_{2}
, and then replace
(x_{1},x_{2})\to(x,x^{\prime})
. In this way, we can easily examine the (anti)symmetry properties of the functions
F
and
G
in (
94
) and extend them to their derivatives.
As a short-hand notation, we may introduce (
n_{1}
,
n_{2}
integers)

\displaystyle(\partial_{1}^{n_{1}}\partial_{2}^{n_{2}}F)(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial^{n_{1}+n_{2}}F}{\partial x_{1}^{n_{1}}\,\partial x_{2}^{n_{2}}}\right)(x_{1}=x,x_{2}=x^{\prime})\,,

\displaystyle(\partial_{1}^{n_{1}}\partial_{2}^{n_{2}}G)(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\left(\frac{\partial^{n_{1}+n_{2}}G}{\partial x_{1}^{n_{1}}\,\partial x_{2}^{n_{2}}}\right)(x_{1}=x,x_{2}=x^{\prime})\,.

(95)

If the quark-gluon-quark correlation functions are assumed to be smooth, then the order of the partial derivatives is arbitrary.

With these notations it is easy to see that the (anti-)symmetry properties of the functions
F
,
G
are inherited by their partial derivatives,

\displaystyle(\partial_{1}^{n_{1}}\partial_{2}^{n_{2}}F)(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\partial_{2}^{n_{1}}\partial_{1}^{n_{2}}F)(x^{\prime},x)\,,

\displaystyle(\partial_{1}^{n_{1}}\partial_{2}^{n_{2}}G)(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(\partial_{2}^{n_{1}}\partial_{1}^{n_{2}}G)(x^{\prime},x)\,.

(96)

Of particular interest is the so-called
soft-gluon pole
, i.e., the region of the support of the functions
F
,
G
where
x^{\prime}=x
. From (
96
) the obvious relations follow:

\displaystyle(\partial_{1}F)(x,x)

\displaystyle=(\partial_{2}F)(x,x),

\displaystyle(\partial^{2}_{1}F)(x,x)

\displaystyle=(\partial^{2}_{2}F)(x,x),

\displaystyle(\partial_{1}\partial_{2}F)(x,x)

\displaystyle=(\partial_{2}\partial_{1}F)(x,x),

\displaystyle(\partial_{1}G)(x,x)

\displaystyle=-(\partial_{2}G)(x,x),

\displaystyle(\partial^{2}_{1}G)(x,x)

\displaystyle=-(\partial^{2}_{2}G)(x,x),

\displaystyle(\partial_{1}\partial_{2}G)(x,x)

\displaystyle=0\,.

(97)

We introduce an even shorter notation for derivative terms along the

diagonal
support,

\displaystyle F^{\prime}(x,x)\equiv\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}x}F(x,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\partial_{1}F+\partial_{2}F)(x,x)=2(\partial_{1}F)(x,x)=2(\partial_{2}F)(x,x),

\displaystyle F^{\prime\prime}(x,x)\equiv\tfrac{\mathrm{d}^{2}}{\mathrm{d}x^{2}}F(x,x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\partial^{2}_{1}F+\partial^{2}_{2}F+\partial_{1}\partial_{2}F+\partial_{2}\partial_{1}F)(x,x).

(98)

Lastly, we discuss the derivatives of antiquark correlation functions
F^{\bar{q}}
,
G^{\bar{q}}
. It is easy to see that the first partial derivatives differ by a sign between quarks and antiquarks, while the second derivatives keep their sign, (
i,j=1,2
),

\displaystyle(\partial_{i}F^{\bar{q}})(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-(\partial_{i}F^{q})(-x,-x^{\prime}),

\displaystyle(\partial_{i}G^{\bar{q}})(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=-(\partial_{i}G^{q})(-x,-x^{\prime})\,,

\displaystyle(\partial_{i}\partial_{j}F^{\bar{q}})(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=+(\partial_{i}\partial_{j}F^{q})(-x,-x^{\prime}),

\displaystyle(\partial_{i}\partial_{j}G^{\bar{q}})(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=+(\partial_{i}\partial_{j}G^{q})(-x,-x^{\prime})\,.

(99)

A.4
Triple-Gluon Correlator

Yet another type of correlator of the nucleon that we need in our calculation is the triple-gluon correlator. It appears in the following two varieties (cf. Refs.
[
71
,
70
]
; note that these references use a different convention for the
\epsilon
-tensor):

\displaystyle N_{F}^{\sigma\tau\rho}(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}\mu}{2\pi}e^{i\lambda x}e^{i\mu(x^{\prime}-x)}\,\times

(100)

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\langle P,S|i\,f^{\alpha\beta\gamma}G^{n\sigma,\alpha}(0)\,[0,\mu\,n]\,ig\,G^{n\rho,\beta}(\mu n)\,[\mu\,n,\lambda\,n]\,G^{n\tau,\gamma}(\lambda n)|P,S\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2iM\left[g_{T}^{\sigma\tau}\epsilon^{\rho PnS}N(x,x^{\prime})-g_{T}^{\tau\rho}\epsilon^{\sigma PnS}N(x,x-x^{\prime})-g_{T}^{\rho\sigma}\epsilon^{\tau PnS}N(x^{\prime},x^{\prime}-x)\right],

\displaystyle O_{F}^{\sigma\tau\rho}(x,x^{\prime})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}\mu}{2\pi}e^{i\lambda x}e^{i\mu(x^{\prime}-x)}\,\times

(101)

\displaystyle\hskip 42.67912pt\langle P,S|\,d^{\alpha\beta\gamma}G^{n\sigma,\alpha}(0)\,[0,\mu\,n]\,ig\,G^{n\rho,\beta}(\mu n)\,[\mu\,n,\lambda\,n]\,G^{n\tau,\gamma}(\lambda n)|P,S\rangle

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 2iM\left[g_{T}^{\sigma\tau}\epsilon^{\rho PnS}O(x,x^{\prime})+g_{T}^{\tau\rho}\epsilon^{\sigma PnS}O(x,x-x^{\prime})+g_{T}^{\rho\sigma}\epsilon^{\tau PnS}O(x^{\prime},x^{\prime}-x)\right],

where
d^{\alpha\gamma\beta}
and
f^{\alpha\gamma\beta}
are the totally symmetric/antisymmetric SU(N) structure constants. We encounter the real-valued functions
N(x,x^{\prime})
and
O(x,x^{\prime})
. They have the same support properties as
F^{q}(x,x^{\prime})
and
G^{q}(x,x^{\prime})
, introduced in the previous subsection,
-1\leq x,x^{\prime}\leq 1
and
|x-x^{\prime}|\leq 1
, and they also have certain symmetry relations. To be specific,
N(x,x^{\prime})=N(x^{\prime},x)=-N(-x,-x^{\prime})
and
O(x,x^{\prime})=O(x^{\prime},x)=O(-x,-x^{\prime})
. Naturally, the contributions to a spin observable generated by the triple-gluon correlators
N_{F}
and
O_{F}
are counted as
dynamical
twist-3 contributions.

A.5
Fragmentation Functions

We further introduce the more familiar parton-to-hadron fragmentation functions which describe the hadronization of a quark or a gluon into a specific hadron. Both fragmentation functions may be provided by the following well-known collinear fragmentation correlators (cf. the review article
[
97
]
on fragmentation functions):

\displaystyle\Delta_{ij}^{h/q}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{N_{c}}\sum_{X}\hskip-14.22636pt\int\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{2\pi}\,\mathrm{e}^{-i\lambda/z}\,\langle\Omega|\,[\infty\,m,0]\,q_{i}(0)\,|P_{h};X\rangle\langle P_{h};X|\,\bar{q}_{j}(\lambda m)\,[\lambda\,m,\infty\,m]\,|\Omega\rangle

(102)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle z^{-1+2\varepsilon}\,\hbox to0.0pt{\hbox to7.80904pt{\hfil/\hfil}\hss}P_{h,ij}\,D_{1}^{h/q}(z)+...\,,

\displaystyle\Delta^{h/g,\mu\nu}(z)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{N_{c}^{2}-1}\sum_{X}\hskip-14.22636pt\int\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{2\pi}\,\mathrm{e}^{-i\lambda/z}\,\langle\Omega|\,G^{m\mu}(0)\,[0,\infty m]\,|P_{h};X\rangle\langle P_{h};X|\,[\infty m,\lambda m]\,G^{m\nu}(\lambda m)\,|\Omega\rangle

(103)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-z^{-2+2\varepsilon}\,g_{\perp}^{\mu\nu}\,D_{1}^{h/g}(z)+...\,.

In these definitions another light-cone vector
m^{\mu}
with
m^{2}=0
,
m\cdot P_{h}=1
is introduced. This light-cone vector may differ from
n^{\mu}
, included in the above definition of nucleon correlators.
P_{h}^{\mu}
and
m^{\mu}
provide a different transverse projector,

g_{\perp}^{\mu\nu}\equiv g^{\mu\nu}-P_{h}^{\mu}m^{\nu}-P_{h}^{\nu}m^{\mu}\,.

(104)

We note that since a hadron is assumed to be observed with momentum
P_{h}^{\mu}
, the summation
\sum_{X}\hskip-14.22636pt\int\,
over intermediate states
|P_{h};X\rangle
is incomplete, which is always the case for a fragmentation process. Furthermore,
|\Omega\rangle
represents the full QCD vacuum state. The definitions (
102
),(
103
) are given in
d=4-2\varepsilon
dimensions. The ellipsis in (
102
),(
103
) indicates other spin-dependent fragmentation functions that are irrelevant for the purpose of this paper.

As explained in Sec.
II.2
, an
\overline{\mathrm{MS}}
-subtraction of UV-divergences emerging in our perturbative calculation is required. It is well understood (see, e.g., the discussion in Refs.
[
36
,
69
]
) how this subtraction works for the leading-twist fragmentation functions, like, e.g., the quark-to-hadron fragmentation function
D_{1,\mathrm{bare}}^{h/q}
that appears in the LO formula (
5
), which is a priori a
bare
, unsubtracted quantity. For the SSA (
1
) at NLO one needs to replace
D_{1,\mathrm{bare}}^{h/q}
by the subtracted,
\overline{\mathrm{MS}}
-renormalized fragmentation function in the following way:

\displaystyle D_{1,\mathrm{bare}}^{h/q}(z,\mu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle D_{1}^{h/q,\overline{\mathrm{MS}}}(z,\mu)+\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu)}{2\pi}\frac{S_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon}(P_{qq}\otimes D_{1}^{h/q,\overline{\mathrm{MS}}})(z,\mu)

(105)

\displaystyle\hskip 65.44142pt+\frac{\alpha_{s}(\mu)}{2\pi}\frac{S_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon}(P_{gq}\otimes D_{1}^{h/g,\overline{\mathrm{MS}}})(z,\mu)\,+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s}^{2})\,,

where
\mu
denotes the renormalization/factorization scale,
S_{\varepsilon}=(4\pi)^{\varepsilon}/\Gamma(1-\varepsilon)
is a convenient prefactor consistent with the
\overline{\mathrm{MS}}
-scheme at NLO, and
P_{qq}
,
P_{gq}
are the well-known LO splitting functions

\displaystyle P_{qq}(w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{F}\left[\frac{1+w^{2}}{(1-w)_{+}}+\frac{3}{2}\delta(1-w)\right]\,,

\displaystyle P_{gq}(w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{F}\,\left[\frac{1+(1-w)^{2}}{w}\right]\,.

(106)

The convolution integral in Eq. (
105
) is defined as usual as

(P\otimes D)(z,\mu)=\int_{z}^{1}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}w}{w}\,P(w)\,D\left(\tfrac{z}{w},\mu\right)\,.

(107)

A.6
Photon-in-Lepton Distribution

Here, we introduce a photon-in-lepton distribution in terms of the following matrix element (cf. Refs.
[
36
,
88
,
69
]
):

\displaystyle\Phi^{\gamma/\ell,\mu\nu}(x)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}\lambda}{2\pi}\,\mathrm{e}^{i\lambda x}\,\langle\ell(l)|\,F^{o\nu}(0)\,F^{o\mu}(\lambda\,o)|\ell(l)\rangle

(108)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{x}{2}\,\frac{\tilde{g}_{T}^{\mu\nu}}{1-\varepsilon}\,f^{\gamma/\ell}(x)+...\,.

The two photon field-strength tensors
F^{o\sigma}(x)=o_{\rho}F^{\rho\sigma}(x)=o_{\rho}(\partial^{\rho}A^{\sigma}(x)-\partial^{\sigma}A^{\rho}(x))
,
A
being the photon field, are to be evaluated between lepton states of momentum
l^{\mu}
. The definition (
108
) utilizes yet another light-cone vector
o^{\mu}
with
o^{2}=0
,
o\cdot l=1
, and the corresponding transverse projector
\tilde{g}_{T}
reads

\tilde{g}_{T}^{\mu\nu}\equiv g^{\mu\nu}-l^{\mu}o^{\nu}-l^{\nu}o^{\mu}\,.

(109)

The special role of the photon-in-lepton distribution
f^{\gamma/\ell}_{1}(x)
is discussed in Sec.
II.3.3
and a discussion of the various light-cone vectors
n^{\mu}
,
m^{\mu}
,
o^{\mu}
can be found below in the next appendix.

We also mention the renormalization of the bare photon-in-lepton distribution
f_{1,\mathrm{bare}}^{\gamma/\ell}(x)
. The distribution
includes a UV-divergence that needs to be
\overline{\mathrm{MS}}
-subtracted. Following Ref.
[
36
]
the
\overline{\mathrm{MS}}
-subtraction term reads

\displaystyle f_{1,\mathrm{bare}}^{\gamma/\ell}(x,\mu)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle f_{1}^{\gamma/\ell,\overline{\mathrm{MS}}}(x,\mu)+\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{em}}}{2\pi}\frac{S_{\varepsilon}}{\varepsilon}\,(P_{\gamma\ell}\otimes f_{1}^{\ell/\ell,\overline{\mathrm{MS}}})(x,\mu)+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{\mathrm{em}}^{2})\,,

(110)

where
P_{\gamma\ell}(w)=P_{gq}(w)/C_{F}
is the lepton-to-photon QED splitting function and
f^{\ell/\ell}(x)
the lepton-in-lepton distribution which, at LO in QED, reduces to
\delta(1-x)
.
In contrast to the QCD parton distributions, the renormalized photon-in-lepton distribution
f_{1}^{\gamma/\ell,\overline{\mathrm{MS}}}(x,\mu)
can be calculated in QED perturbation theory, and one obtains
[
36
]
,

f_{1}^{\gamma/\ell,\overline{\mathrm{MS}}}(x,\mu)=\frac{\alpha_{\mathrm{em}}}{2\pi}\,P_{\gamma\ell}(x)\,\left[\ln\left(\frac{\mu^{2}}{x^{2}\,m_{\ell}^{2}}\right)-1\right]+\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{\mathrm{em}}^{2})\,.

(111)

Note the explicit dependence on the lepton mass
m_{\ell}
in the logarithm in (
111
). Since the renormalization/factorization scale
\mu
is typically of the order of the hard scale of the underlying process, that is, of the order of several GeV at least, the logarithm
\ln\left(\frac{\mu^{2}}{x^{2}\,m_{\ell}^{2}}\right)
can potentially become large and its resummation to all orders may be necessary. More discussion on the treatment of the lepton mass
m_{\ell}
in (
111
) is included in Sec.
II.3.3
.

Appendix B
Discussion on the Choice of Gauge and Related Subjects

In this appendix we discuss the gauge conditions which we impose throughout this paper. In addition, we address the special role of the light-cone vectors
n^{\mu},m^{\mu},o^{\mu}
,
which were featured in several of the definitions in the previous Appendix
A
for the (multiparton) correlation functions and fragmentation functions
(see Eqs. (
90
),(
92
),(
93
),(
100
),(
101
),(
103
), and(
108
)).

B.1
Light-Cone Gauge

First, we point out that we organize our calculation of the partonic hard factors in the collinear twist-3 factorization approach using a specific light-cone gauge for
the gluonic field
G^{\mu,\alpha}(x)
(
\alpha
being a color index in the adjoint representation) with asymmetric boundary conditions,

n_{\mu}G^{\mu,\alpha}(x)=0\quad\mathrm{and}\quad G^{\alpha}_{T}(P\cdot x=+\infty)+G^{\alpha}_{T}(P\cdot x=-\infty)=0\,.

(112)

This gauge is a pragmatic choice already adopted in a similar, yet simpler NLO calculation for the production of polarized
\Lambda
particles in electron-positron collisions
[
52
]
. It leads to simplifications since the gluonic field-strength tensors
G^{n\rho}
in the definitions (
92
),(
93
),(
100
), and (
101
) reduce to
(n\cdot\partial)G_{T}^{\rho}
, which can be easily inverted. In addition, the Wilson lines in these definitions reduce to unity. All of this simplifies the handling of the quark-gluon-quark and triple-gluon correlations.

In addition, the matrix element (
90
) simplifies in the light-cone gauge (
112
). However, we must be careful when applying this gauge to the Sivers function
f_{1T}^{\perp}
. As described in Ref.
[
72
]
, in light-cone gauge the Sivers function is generated by
transverse
Wilson lines at light-cone infinity, and, the boundary conditions are important. According to Ref.
[
98
]
, the asymmetric boundary condition in (
112
) guarantees a non-zero Sivers function for both a DIS-like process (like the single-inclusive hadron production
\ell N\to hX
) and a Drell-Yan (DY) like process. However, the Sivers functions in DIS and DY
differ by a sign
[
99
]
. In turn, the identity (
10
), derived for a DIS-type process in Ref.
[
72
]
,
requires a change of sign on the r.h.s. for a DY-type process.

However, there is a price to pay when using the light-cone gauge (
112
). Gluonic polarization sums as well as the numerator of gluonic Feynman propagators
\--
both objects that copiously appear in the calculation of partonic hard factors at NLO
\--
are more complicated in this gauge compared to covariant Feynman gauge. It is well known that both must be modified in light-cone gauge (
112
) according to

-g^{\mu\nu}\to-d^{\mu\nu}(k,n)\equiv-\left(g^{\mu\nu}-\kappa\,\mathcal{P}\frac{k^{\mu}n^{\nu}+k^{\nu}n^{\mu}}{k\cdot n}\right)\,.

(113)

The parameter
\kappa
allows us to switch between Feynman gauge (
\kappa=0
) and light-cone gauge (
\kappa=1
). It turns out that all terms proportional to
\kappa
in the final result for the partonic hard factors cancel upon application of the identity (
10
). This means that our results for partonic hard factors coincide in Feynman gauge and in light-cone gauge. We take this as an indication that all partonic hard factors in the collinear twist-3 approach to the SSA (
1
) are color gauge invariant. Hence, in turn, this means that we can use the gauge-invariant extensions of the twist-3 matrix elements (
90
),(
93
) in our collinear twist-3 approach.

However, we emphasize that the choice of a light-cone gauge (
112
) is certainly not a necessity for an NLO calculation; one may also choose the Feynman gauge right away.

B.2
Choice of the Light-Cone Vector

Secondly, as discussed in Sect.
II.1
, there is an additional potential dependence on the light-cone vector
n^{\mu}
of physical observables through the parameterizations (
90
),(
92
),(
93
),(
100
),(
101
). In Ref.
[
43
]
it was shown for the LO contribution to the SSA (
1
) that an arbitrary light-cone vector
n^{\mu}
, parameterized by the three external physical vectors of the process, can be adopted. Applying the identity (
10
), one finds that
all dependences on
n^{\mu}
cancel in the final LO result (
5
). However, at NLO, working with an arbitrary light-cone vector
n^{\mu}
would require the computation of numerous redundant terms that are eventually expected to disappear. In order to avoid this, we take the LO finding of Ref.
[
43
]
for granted in our NLO calculation, and assume a specific light-cone vector right away from the beginning,

n^{\mu}=-\tfrac{2}{t}P_{h}^{\mu}\,.

(114)

This would correspond to a natural choice in a specific frame in which the nucleon momentum
P^{\mu}
and the momentum
P_{h}^{\mu}
of the produced hadron are collinear along one axis, e.g. the
z
-axis. We assume that any arbitrariness of the choice (
114
) drops out at NLO once the constraint (
10
) is applied.

Appendix C
Analytic Continuation and Useful Integration by Parts Identities

In the calculation of several of the contributions, we find terms of the form
1/(1-w)^{n+\varepsilon}
that require careful treatment with respect to the soft limit
w\to 1
.
A priori
such terms are only integrable on an interval
(x_{0},1)
under the condition that
\varepsilon<-(n-1)
. However, integration by parts can be applied to find an analytic continuation of such terms that extends also to the region
-(n-1)<\varepsilon<0
. Although we only need the specific cases
n=2
and
n=3
in our calculation, we note that a closed form can also be found for a general integer
n
. Let
f
be a sufficiently differentiable function satisfying the boundary conditions
f(1)=f^{\prime}(1)=\dots=f^{(n-2)}(1)=0
(where
f^{(n)}
denotes the
n
-th derivative) and let
x_{0}
be a real constant with
0<x_{0}<1
. Then the following general analytic continuation holds

\int_{x_{0}}^{1}\mathrm{d}w\frac{f\left(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w}\right)}{(1-w)^{n+\varepsilon}}\,=\,\int_{x_{0}}^{1}\mathrm{d}w\frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{(1-w)^{1+\varepsilon}}\frac{\Gamma(1+\varepsilon)}{\Gamma(n+\varepsilon)}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{n-1}}{\mathrm{d}w^{n-1}}f\left(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w}\right)\,.

(115)

This can be shown in a straightforward way by induction. The induction step from
n
to
n+1
looks like this:

\displaystyle\int_{x_{0}}^{1}\mathrm{d}w\frac{f\left(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w}\right)}{(1-w)^{n+1+\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{(1-w)^{-n-\varepsilon}}{-n-\varepsilon}f\left(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w}\right)\bigg|_{x_{0}}^{1}+\int_{x_{0}}^{1}\mathrm{d}w\frac{(1-w)^{-n-\varepsilon}}{-n-\varepsilon}\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}w}f\left(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w}\right)

(116)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt=\int_{x_{0}}^{1}\mathrm{d}w\frac{-1}{n+\varepsilon}\frac{(-1)^{n-1}}{(1-w)^{1+\varepsilon}}\frac{\Gamma(1+\varepsilon)}{\Gamma(n+\varepsilon)}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{n-1}}{\mathrm{d}w^{n-1}}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}w}f\left(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w}\right)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt=\int_{x_{0}}^{1}\mathrm{d}w\frac{(-1)^{n}}{(1-w)^{1+\varepsilon}}\frac{\Gamma(1+\varepsilon)}{\Gamma(n+1+\varepsilon)}\frac{\mathrm{d}^{n}}{\mathrm{d}w^{n}}f\left(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w}\right)\,,

where the boundary term at
w=1
vanishes because
\varepsilon<-n
and the boundary term at
w=x_{0}
because
f(1)=0
. We emphasize that only after the analytic continuation (
115
) has been applied can one proceed with the small-
\varepsilon
expansion and make use of the familiar relation between
(1-w)^{-1-\varepsilon}
and the
\delta
- and plus-distributions given by Eq. (
20
).

For completeness we also give the explicit formulas for the specific cases
n=2
and
n=3
that were encountered in our calculation, with the generic function
f
replaced by other relevant
objects.

C.1
Kinematical Contributions

For the analytic continuation of the term
1/(1-w)^{2+2\varepsilon}
appearing in the computation of kinematical contributions, for example in the
qg\to q
-channel, it is convenient to use the following identity:

\displaystyle\int_{x_{0}}^{1}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}w}{w}\,\frac{\sigma_{1}(v,w,\varepsilon)\,f_{1T}^{\perp(1)}(\frac{x_{0}}{w})}{(1-w)^{2+2\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{1}{1+2\varepsilon}\int_{x_{0}}^{1}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}w}{w}\frac{[(\partial_{w}\sigma_{1})-\frac{1}{w}\sigma_{1}](v,w,\varepsilon)\,f_{1T}^{\perp(1)}(\frac{x_{0}}{w})}{(1-w)^{1+2\varepsilon}}

(117)

\displaystyle+\frac{1}{1+2\varepsilon}\int_{x_{0}}^{1}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}w}{w}\frac{\frac{1}{w}\sigma_{1}(v,w,\varepsilon)\,\frac{x_{0}}{w}(f_{1T}^{\perp(1)})^{\prime}(\frac{x_{0}}{w})}{(1-w)^{1+2\varepsilon}}\,.

C.2
Hard Poles

For the calculation of the hard-pole contributions in the
qg\to q
-channel, that is, the partonic factors accompanying the functions
F\left(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w},x_{0}\right)
and
G\left(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w},x_{0}\right)
, we apply the following identities:

\displaystyle\int_{x_{0}}^{1}\mathrm{d}w\,\frac{\sigma_{3}(v,\varepsilon)}{(1-w)^{3+\varepsilon}}F(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w},x_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma_{3}(v,\varepsilon)}{(1+\varepsilon)(2+\varepsilon)}\times

(118)

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt\int_{x_{0}}^{1}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}w}{w}\,\frac{\frac{1}{w^{3}}\left(x_{0}^{2}\,\partial_{1}^{2}F\right)(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w},x_{0})-\frac{2}{w^{2}}\left(-x_{0}\,\partial_{1}F\right)(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w},x_{0})}{(1-w)^{1+\varepsilon}}\,,

\displaystyle\int_{x_{0}}^{1}\mathrm{d}w\,\frac{\sigma_{2}(v,\varepsilon)}{(1-w)^{2+\varepsilon}}F(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w},x_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\sigma_{2}(v,\varepsilon)}{1+\varepsilon}\int_{x_{0}}^{1}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}w}{w}\,\frac{\frac{1}{w}\left(-x_{0}\,\partial_{1}F\right)(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w},x_{0})}{(1-w)^{1+\varepsilon}}\,.

(119)

The right-hand-sides of Eqs. (
118
),(
119
) can then be expanded in
\varepsilon
using (
20
).
Note that the above procedure works for both correlation functions
F
and
G
.
The hard-pole subtraction terms in (
31
),(
32
) also contain contributions of other derivative terms
-x_{0}\,(\partial_{2}F)(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w},x_{0})
and
-x_{0}\,(\partial_{2}G)(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w},x_{0})
. These terms need to be dealt with in the same way as described in Eq. (
119
), followed by application of (
20
), and (only afterwards) expansion in
\varepsilon
.
We note that all our results are organized in such a way that derivative terms are integrated by parts as much as possible. Integration by parts identities are applied to all regular, non-distributional terms of the corresponding partonic cross sections. As a consequence, only delta- and plus distributions remain in our results for the partonic factors that accompany derivatives of the
qgq
functions. This procedure is performed using the following list of useful formulas:

\displaystyle\int_{x_{0}}^{1}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}w}{w}\,\sigma(w)\,x_{0}^{2}\,(\partial_{1}^{2}F)(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w},x_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-(\sigma(1)+\sigma^{\prime}(1))\,F(x_{0},x_{0})+\tfrac{1}{2}\sigma(1)\,(-x_{0}\,F^{\prime}(x_{0},x_{0}))

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt+\int_{x_{0}}^{1}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}w}{w}\,\left[2w^{2}\,\sigma(w)+4w^{3}\,\sigma^{\prime}(w)+w^{4}\,\sigma^{\prime\prime}(w)\right]\,F(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w},x_{0})\,,

\displaystyle\int_{x_{0}}^{1}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}w}{w}\,\sigma(w)\,(-x_{0})\,(\partial_{1}F)(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w},x_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma(1)\,F(x_{0},x_{0})-\int_{x_{0}}^{1}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}w}{w}\left[w\,\sigma(w)+w^{2}\,\sigma^{\prime}(w)\right]\,F(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w},x_{0})\,,

\displaystyle\int_{x_{0}}^{1}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}w}{w}\,\tfrac{\sigma(v)\,\ln(1-w)}{w}\,(-x_{0})\,(\partial_{1}F)(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w},x_{0})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma(v)\int_{x_{0}}^{1}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}w}{w}\,\tfrac{F(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w},x_{0})}{(1-w)_{+}}-\sigma(v)\int_{x_{0}}^{1}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}w}{w}\,F(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w},x_{0})\,.

(120)

C.3
Soft-Gluon Poles

Additional complications arise for the NLO computation of the SGP contributions (in the
qg\to q
channel). Let us focus on the SGP subtraction terms in the third lines of Eqs. (
31
),(
32
). As for the hard poles, the all-order (in
\varepsilon
) results for the partonic cross sections involve terms
1/(1-w)^{3+\varepsilon}
,
1/(1-w)^{2+\varepsilon}
that we need to deal with via Eq. (
115
). This implies the following two identities:

\displaystyle\int_{x_{0}}^{1}\mathrm{d}w\,\frac{\sigma_{3}(v,\varepsilon)\,F(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w},\tfrac{x_{0}}{w})}{(1-w)^{3+\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{\sigma_{3}(v,\varepsilon)}{(2+\varepsilon)(1+\varepsilon)}\,\int_{x_{0}}^{1}\mathrm{d}w\,\frac{\frac{1}{w^{4}}\,(x_{0}^{2}\,F^{\prime\prime}(\frac{x_{0}}{w},\frac{x_{0}}{w}))-\frac{2}{w^{3}}\,(-x_{0}\,F^{\prime}(\frac{x_{0}}{w},\frac{x_{0}}{w}))}{(1-w)^{1+\varepsilon}}\,,

\displaystyle\int_{x_{0}}^{1}\mathrm{d}w\,\frac{\sigma_{2}(v,\varepsilon)\,F(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w},\tfrac{x_{0}}{w})}{(1-w)^{2+\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\sigma_{2}(v,\varepsilon)}{1+\varepsilon}\int_{x_{0}}^{1}\mathrm{d}w\,\frac{\frac{1}{w^{2}}(-x_{0}\,F^{\prime}(\frac{x_{0}}{w},\frac{x_{0}}{w}))}{(1-w)^{1+\varepsilon}}\,.

(121)

However, the all-
\varepsilon
-order results for the partonic cross sections also contain terms that are proportional to a hypergeometric function which, interestingly, comes with a term
1/(1-w)^{2+2\varepsilon}
,

\frac{{}_{2}F_{1}(-\varepsilon,-\varepsilon;1-\varepsilon;\tfrac{w}{1-v+v\,w})}{(1-w)^{2+2\varepsilon}}.

This term needs extra care. We again perform an integration by parts, which in turn requires a derivative of the hypergeometric function. We use the following identity for this derivative:

\tfrac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}w}{}_{2}F_{1}(-\varepsilon,-\varepsilon;1-\varepsilon;\tfrac{w}{1-v+v\,w})=\varepsilon\,\tfrac{1-v}{w(1-v+v\,w)}\,_{2}F_{1}(-\varepsilon,-\varepsilon;1-\varepsilon;\tfrac{w}{1-v+v\,w})-\varepsilon\,\tfrac{(1-v)^{1+\varepsilon}(1-w)^{\varepsilon}}{w\,(1-v+v\,w)^{1+\varepsilon}}.

By means of this identity we derive the following integral:

\displaystyle\int_{x_{0}}^{1}\mathrm{d}w\,\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{2}(v,\varepsilon)\,_{2}F_{1}(-\varepsilon,-\varepsilon;1-\varepsilon;\tfrac{w}{1-v+vw})\,F(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w},\tfrac{x_{0}}{w})}{(1-w)^{2+2\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\tilde{\sigma}_{2}(v,\varepsilon)}{1+2\varepsilon}\int_{x_{0}}^{1}\mathrm{d}w\times

(122)

\displaystyle\hskip-284.52756pt\Bigg[\frac{\frac{1}{w^{2}}\,_{2}F_{1}(-\varepsilon,-\varepsilon;1-\varepsilon;\tfrac{w}{1-v+vw})\,(-x_{0}\,F^{\prime}(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w},\tfrac{x_{0}}{w}))}{(1-w)^{1+2\varepsilon}}-\frac{\varepsilon\,(1-v)^{1+\varepsilon}\,F(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w},\tfrac{x_{0}}{w})}{(1-w)^{1+\varepsilon}\,w\,(1-v+vw)^{1+\varepsilon}}

\displaystyle\hskip-170.71652pt+\frac{\frac{1-v}{w\,(1-v+vw)}\,\varepsilon\,_{2}F_{1}(-\varepsilon,-\varepsilon;1-\varepsilon;\tfrac{w}{1-v+vw})\,F(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w},\tfrac{x_{0}}{w})}{(1-w)^{1+2\varepsilon}}\Bigg]\,.

These expressions can now safely be expanded in
\varepsilon
by virtue of Eq. (
20
) (with a replacement
\varepsilon\to 2\varepsilon
where needed), and an expansion of the hypergeometric function

{}_{2}F_{1}(-\varepsilon,-\varepsilon;1-\varepsilon;\tfrac{w}{1-v+vw})=1+\varepsilon^{2}\,\mathrm{Li}_{2}(\tfrac{w}{1-v+vw})+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{3})\,.

Another apparent complication in the calculation of the all-
\varepsilon
-order results for the partonic cross sections is that another special function emerges, the Appell hypergeometric function of two arguments,

F_{1}(-2\varepsilon;-\varepsilon,-\varepsilon;1-2\varepsilon;\tfrac{vw}{1-v+vw};-\tfrac{v(1-w)}{1-v+vw})=1+\mathcal{O}(\varepsilon^{3})\,.

Since this function is typically accompanied by a factor
1/(1-w)^{\varepsilon}
no dangerous behavior as
w\to 1
is observed, and one can readily replace the Appell function by unity.

The partonic cross sections for the derivative parts
F^{\prime}(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w},\tfrac{x_{0}}{w})
,
(\partial_{2}G)(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w},\tfrac{x_{0}}{w})
of the SGP subtraction terms in Eq. (
31
),(
32
) are accompanied only by a factor
1/(1-w)^{2+\varepsilon}
and can be handled using (
121
),(
122
) in a similar way.

As for the HP contributions, we list some useful formulas that facilitate the integration by parts of all regular, non-distributional terms in the partonic factors accompanying the derivatives of
qgq
functions. For the SGP contributions, we make use of the following identities,

\displaystyle\int^{1}_{x_{0}}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}w}{w}\,\sigma(w)\,\left(-x_{0}\,F^{\prime}(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w},\tfrac{x_{0}}{w})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma(1)\,F(x_{0},x_{0})-\int^{1}_{x_{0}}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}w}{w}\,w\left(w\,\sigma(w)\right)^{\prime}\,F(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w},\tfrac{x_{0}}{w})\,,

\displaystyle\int^{1}_{x_{0}}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}w}{w}\,\sigma(w)\,\ln(1-w)\,\left(-x_{0}\,F^{\prime}(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w},\tfrac{x_{0}}{w})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int^{1}_{x_{0}}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}w}{w}\,\frac{w^{2}\,\sigma(w)}{(1-w)_{+}}\,F(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w},\tfrac{x_{0}}{w})-\int^{1}_{x_{0}}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}w}{w}\,w\left(w\,\sigma(w)\right)^{\prime}\,\ln(1-w)\,F(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w},\tfrac{x_{0}}{w})\,,

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\int^{1}_{x_{0}}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}w}{w}\,\sigma(w)\,\left(x^{2}_{0}\,F^{\prime\prime}(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w},\tfrac{x_{0}}{w})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma(1)\,\left(-x_{0}\,F^{\prime}(x_{0},x_{0})\right)-(w\,\sigma(w))^{\prime}\Big|_{w=1}\,F(x_{0},x_{0})+\int^{1}_{x_{0}}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}w}{w}\,w\,[w^{2}\,(w\,\sigma(w))^{\prime}]^{\prime}\,F(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w},\tfrac{x_{0}}{w})\,,

\displaystyle\int^{1}_{x_{0}}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}w}{w}\,\sigma(w)\,\left(x^{2}_{0}\,(\partial_{1}^{2}G)(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w},\tfrac{x_{0}}{w})\right)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sigma(1)\,\left(-x_{0}\,(\partial_{1}G)(x_{0},x_{0})\right)-\int^{1}_{x_{0}}\tfrac{\mathrm{d}w}{w}\,w\left(w\,\sigma(w)\right)^{\prime}\,\left(-x_{0}\,(\partial_{1}G)(\tfrac{x_{0}}{w},\tfrac{x_{0}}{w})\right)\,.

(123)

Appendix D
Analytical Results for the Partonic Hard-Scattering Factors at NLO

In this appendix, we present the explicit analytical results for the various partonic factors.

D.1
Channel
qg\to q

D.1.1
Integral Contribution

The partonic factor accompanying the vector-type correlation function
F
in our result for the integral contribution to the
qg\to q
-channel in Eq. (
64
) reads explicitly,

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{Int}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w,\zeta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{w}{4(1-v)^{4}}\Bigg(\frac{(N_{c}-2\,C_{F})\,\zeta\,(1-v)^{2}v(w-\zeta)^{2}(1+v(w-\zeta))}{\sqrt{1-2\zeta v+2(2\zeta-1)vw+v^{2}(w-\zeta)^{2}}}

(124)

\displaystyle-2C_{F}\,v(w-\zeta)^{2}\,\Big(\zeta^{2}+2(\zeta-1)\zeta^{2}v^{3}w\left(6w^{2}-8w+3\right)

\displaystyle+2\zeta v\left(-\zeta+\left(\zeta^{2}+3\zeta-3\right)w+1\right)

\displaystyle+v^{2}\left(\zeta^{2}+\left(8\zeta^{3}-4\zeta^{2}-2\right)w^{2}+2\zeta\left(-4\zeta^{2}+2\zeta+1\right)w\right)

\displaystyle+\mathrm{sgn}(w-\zeta)\left(\zeta(\zeta+1)+v^{2}\left(\zeta^{2}+\zeta+2w^{2}-2\zeta w\right)+2\zeta v(w-\zeta)\right)\Big)

\displaystyle-N_{c}\,\Big(v(w-\zeta)^{2}\left(\zeta(\zeta+2)+v^{2}\left(\zeta^{2}+\left(4\zeta^{2}-2\right)w^{2}+2(1-2\zeta)\zeta w\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+v\left(\left(4\zeta^{2}-2\right)w-2\zeta^{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\mathrm{sgn}(w-\zeta)\left(v^{3}(w-\zeta)^{2}\left((\zeta-1)\zeta+2w^{2}-2\zeta w\right)\right.

\displaystyle+2v^{2}\left(-(\zeta-1)\zeta^{3}+w^{3}-\zeta(\zeta+1)w^{2}+\zeta\left(3\zeta^{2}-\zeta-1\right)w\right)

\displaystyle\left.+\zeta(\zeta+1)v(w-\zeta)^{2}+2\zeta\left(\zeta^{2}-1\right)w\right)\Big)\Bigg)\,,

where
\mathrm{sgn}(x)
is the sign function. Furthermore, the partonic factor of the axial-vector type correlation function
G
in Eq.
64
reads,

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{Int}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w,\zeta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{w}{4(1-v)^{4}}\Bigg(\frac{(N_{c}-2\,C_{F})\,\zeta(1-v)^{2}v(w-\zeta)^{2}(1+v(w-\zeta))}{\sqrt{1-2\zeta v+2(2\zeta-1)vw+v^{2}(w-\zeta)^{2}}}

(125)

\displaystyle+2C_{F}\,v(w-\zeta)^{2}\Big(\zeta^{2}+2(\zeta-1)\zeta^{2}v^{3}w\left(6w^{2}-8w+3\right)

\displaystyle+2\zeta v\left(-\zeta+\left(\zeta^{2}-\zeta+1\right)w-1\right)

\displaystyle+v^{2}\left(\zeta^{2}+\left(8\zeta^{3}-4\zeta^{2}-2\right)w^{2}+2\zeta\left(-4\zeta^{2}+2\zeta+1\right)w\right)

\displaystyle+\mathrm{sgn}(w-\zeta)\left((\zeta-1)\zeta+v^{2}\left((\zeta-1)\zeta+2w^{2}-2\zeta w\right)+2\zeta v(w-\zeta)\right)\Big)

\displaystyle+N_{c}\,\Big(v(w-\zeta)^{2}\left((\zeta-2)\zeta+v^{2}\left(\zeta^{2}+\left(4\zeta^{2}-2\right)w^{2}+2(1-2\zeta)\zeta w\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+v\left(\left(4\zeta^{2}-2\right)w-2\zeta^{2}\right)\right)

\displaystyle+\mathrm{sgn}(w-\zeta)\left(v^{3}(w-\zeta)^{2}\left(\zeta^{2}+\zeta+2w^{2}-2\zeta w\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+2v^{2}\left(-\zeta^{3}(\zeta+1)+w^{3}-\zeta(\zeta+3)w^{2}+\zeta\left(3\zeta^{2}+\zeta+1\right)w\right)\right.

\displaystyle\left.+(\zeta-1)\zeta v(w-\zeta)^{2}+2(\zeta-1)^{2}\zeta w\right)\Big)\Bigg)\,.

D.1.2
Hard-Pole Contribution

Here we list the explicit analytic results for the partonic factors that appear in our formula for the hard-pole contribution to the
qg\to q
-channel in Eq. (
65
),

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{HP},F}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w,\chi_{\mu})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{F}\Bigg[\frac{\left(v^{2}-14v+18\right)\ln(1-v)}{v^{2}\,(1-v)^{2}\,(1-w)_{+}}

(126)

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt-\frac{75v^{5}-100v^{4}+151v^{3}-1720v^{2}+2460v-1080}{60\,v\,(1-v)^{4}\,(1-w)_{+}}+\frac{2(2v-3)\,\ln(1-v)}{v^{2}\,w\,(1-v)^{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt+\frac{v^{5}(75w-9)+v^{4}(22-130w)+v^{3}(7-30w)-20v^{2}(w+12)+390v-180}{30\,v\,w\,(1-v)^{4}}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip-71.13188pt+N_{c}\,\Bigg[\frac{1+v^{2}}{(1-v)^{4}}\left(\frac{\ln(1-w)}{1-w}\right)_{+}+\frac{\left(3v^{4}+16v^{3}-43v^{2}+50v-18\right)\ln(1-v)}{2v^{2}(1-v)^{4}\,(1-w)_{+}}

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt+\frac{(1+v^{2})\ln(\chi_{\mu})}{(1-v)^{4}\,(1-w)_{+}}+\frac{16v^{4}-85v^{3}-176v^{2}+133v-108}{12v(1-v)^{4}\,(1-w)_{+}}

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt-\frac{\left(v^{4}w+2v^{3}+v^{2}(w-7)+8v-3\right)\ln(1-v)}{v^{2}\,(1-v)^{4}w}-\frac{\left(1+v^{2}\right)(\ln(1-w)+\ln(\chi_{\mu}))}{2\,(1-v)^{4}}

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt+\frac{18+4v^{2}(6+w)-v^{4}(13w-1)}{6v\,(1-v)^{4}w}+\frac{3\left(1+v^{2}\right)w\left(w^{2}+2w-1\right)\ln(w)}{6\,(1-v)^{4}w\,(1-w)^{2}}

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt-\frac{\left(35w^{2}-76w+35+v^{2}\left(45w^{2}-53w+2\right)\right)}{6\,(1-v)^{4}w\,(1-w)}\Bigg].

Note that the last two terms combined
\--
although individually divergent
\--
are finite for
w\to 1
.

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{HP},\partial_{1}F}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w,\chi_{\mu})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{F}\Bigg[-\frac{27v^{5}-44v^{4}+291v^{3}-1600v^{2}+2040v-840}{60\,v\,(1-v)^{4}\,(1-w)_{+}}

(127)

\displaystyle\hskip 28.45274pt+\frac{\left(v^{2}-13v+14\right)\ln(1-v)}{v^{2}\,(1-v)^{2}\,(1-w)_{+}}\Bigg]

\displaystyle\hskip-71.13188pt+N_{c}\,\Bigg[\frac{1+v^{2}}{(1-v)^{4}}\left(\frac{\ln(1-w)}{1-w}\right)_{+}+\frac{\left(3v^{4}+15v^{3}-37v^{2}+41v-14\right)\ln(1-v)}{2\,v^{2}\,(1-v)^{4}\,(1-w)_{+}}

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt+\frac{\left(1+v^{2}\right)\ln(\chi_{\mu})}{(1-v)^{4}\,(1-w)_{+}}+\frac{6v^{4}-51v^{3}-142v^{2}+115v-84}{12\,v\,(1-v)^{4}\,(1-w)_{+}}\Bigg].

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{HP},\partial^{2}_{1}F}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{HP},\partial_{1}\partial_{2}F}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)

(128)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle C_{F}\Bigg[\frac{\ln(1-v)}{v^{2}\,(1-v)\,(1-w)_{+}}-\frac{2v^{5}-5v^{4}+22v^{3}-125v^{2}+150v-60}{60\,v\,(1-v)^{4}\,(1-w)_{+}}\Bigg]

D.1.3
Soft-Gluon Pole Contribution

In the following passage, the partonic factors of Eq. (
66
) are listed, which constitutes our result for the soft-gluon pole contribution to the
qg\to q
-channel,

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SGP},F}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w,\chi_{\mu},\chi_{m})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle+C_{F}\,\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SGP},F,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w,\chi_{\mu},\chi_{m})\,,

where we divided the partonic cross section into its contributions entering with various color factors. The
N_{c}
contribution reads,

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SGP},F,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w,\chi_{\mu},\chi_{m})

\displaystyle A_{1,\mathrm{SGP},F^{\prime},C_{F}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,\chi_{\mu})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1+v^{2}}{(1-v)^{4}}\ln(v)\,\left(4\ln(1-v)+2\ln(\chi_{\mu})-\ln(v)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt+\frac{11v^{4}+15v^{3}-29v^{2}+41v-14}{2\,v^{2}\,(1-v)^{4}}\ln(1-v)+3\frac{1+v^{2}}{(1-v)^{4}}\,\left(\ln(\chi_{\mu})-\ln(v)\right)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt+\frac{27v^{5}-44v^{4}-669v^{3}-1600v^{2}+1080v-840}{120\,v\,(1-v)^{4}}\,,

\displaystyle A_{2,\mathrm{SGP},F^{\prime},C_{F}}^{qg\to q,1}(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 8\frac{1+v^{2}}{(1-v)^{4}}\,,

\displaystyle A_{3,\mathrm{SGP},F^{\prime},C_{F}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,\chi_{\mu})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 4\frac{1+v^{2}}{(1-v)^{4}}\ln(\chi_{\mu})+\frac{14v^{4}+23v^{3}-44v^{2}+59v-20}{2\,v^{2}\,(1-v)^{4}}\ln(1-v)

(143)

\displaystyle+\frac{12v^{5}-17v^{4}+154v^{3}-775v^{2}+980v-400}{40\,v\,(1-v)^{4}}\,.

The partonic cross sections for the terms with the second derivative have similar forms. Again we perform a separation into color structures,

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SGP},F^{\prime\prime}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N_{c}\,\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SGP},F^{\prime\prime},N_{c}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)+C_{F}\,\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SGP},F^{\prime\prime},C_{F}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)\,,

(144)

where the
N_{c}
part reads,

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SGP},F^{\prime\prime},N_{c}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A_{1,\mathrm{SGP},F^{\prime\prime},N_{c}}^{qg\to q,1}(v)\,\left(\delta(1-w)+\frac{1}{(1-w)_{+}}\right)\,,

(145)

with the coefficient function

\displaystyle A_{1,\mathrm{SGP},F^{\prime\prime},N_{c}}^{qg\to q,1}(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{4\,v^{2}\,(1-v)}\ln(1-v)-\frac{v^{4}-4v^{3}-19v^{2}+20v-12}{48\,v\,(1-v)^{4}}\,.

(146)

The
C_{F}
part reads

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SGP},F^{\prime\prime},C_{F}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A_{1,\mathrm{SGP},F^{\prime\prime},C_{F}}^{qg\to q,1}(v)\,\left(\delta(1-w)+\frac{1}{(1-w)_{+}}\right)\,,

(147)

with the coefficient function

\displaystyle A_{1,\mathrm{SGP},F^{\prime\prime},C_{F}}^{qg\to q,1}(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{\ln(1-v)}{2\,v^{2}\,(1-v)}+\frac{2v^{5}-5v^{4}+22v^{3}-125v^{2}+150v-60}{120\,v\,(1-v)^{4}}\,.

(148)

The other partonic cross section related to a second derivative term in Eq. (
66
) is
\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SGP},\partial_{1}^{2}F}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)
. It shows the least analytical complexity and reads

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SGP},\partial_{1}^{2}F}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\delta(1-w)\,\Bigg[N_{c}\,\left(-\frac{\ln(1-v)}{4\,v^{2}\,(1-v)}+\frac{v^{4}-4v^{3}-19v^{2}+20v-12}{48\,v\,(1-v)^{4}}\right)

(149)

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt+C_{F}\,\left(\frac{\ln(1-v)}{2\,v^{2}\,(1-v)}-\frac{2v^{5}-5v^{4}+22v^{3}-125v^{2}+150v-60}{120\,v\,(1-v)^{4}}\right)\Bigg].

We next turn to the terms in Eq. (
66
) that are generated by the quark-gluon-quark correlation function
G

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SGP},\partial_{1}G}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)

\displaystyle N_{c}\,\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SGP},\partial_{1}G,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)+C_{F}\,\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SGP},\partial_{1}G,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)\,,

(150)

where we again divided the partonic cross section into its color factor contributions. The
N_{c}
contribution reads,

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SGP},\partial_{1}G,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A_{1,\mathrm{SGP},\partial_{1}G,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,5}(v)\,\delta(1-w)+A_{2,\mathrm{SGP},\partial_{1}G,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,5}(v)\,\frac{1}{(1-w)_{+}}

(151)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt+A_{3,\mathrm{SGP},\partial_{1}G,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)\,\ln(1-w)+A_{4,\mathrm{SGP},\partial_{1}G,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,5}(v)\,\ln(1-v+v\,w)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt+A_{5,\mathrm{SGP},\partial_{1}G,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)\,\ln(1-v)+A_{6,\mathrm{SGP},\partial_{1}G,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)\,,

with six coefficient functions of the following explicit form:

\displaystyle A_{1,\mathrm{SGP},\partial_{1}G,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,5}(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{v^{2}-13v+14}{2\,v^{2}\,(1-v)^{2}}\ln(1-v)-\frac{6v^{4}-39v^{3}+150v^{2}-209v+84}{12\,v\,(1-v)^{4}}\,,

\displaystyle A_{2,\mathrm{SGP},\partial_{1}G,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,5}(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{8-7v}{2\,v^{2}\,(1-v)^{2}}\ln(1-v)+\frac{9v^{4}-34v^{3}+155v^{2}-228v+96}{24\,v\,(1-v)^{4}}\,,

\displaystyle A_{3,\mathrm{SGP},\partial_{1}G,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)

\displaystyle\frac{v^{2}\left(v\left(w^{2}-w+1\right)+w-2\right)}{(1-v)^{4}}\,,

\displaystyle A_{4,\mathrm{SGP},\partial_{1}G,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,5}(v)

\displaystyle-\frac{v}{2(1-v)^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle A_{6,\mathrm{SGP},\partial_{1}G,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{9v^{4}-34v^{3}+155v^{2}-228v+96}{24\,v\,(1-v)^{4}}

(152)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt-\frac{w\,(v^{4}-2v^{3}+73v^{2}-128v+60)}{12\,v\,(1-v)^{4}}-\frac{w^{2}\,(7v^{3}-6v^{2}-v-8)}{24\,(1-v)^{4}}\,.

The
C_{F}

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SGP},\partial_{1}G,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)

with six coefficient functions of the following explicit form:

\displaystyle A_{1,\mathrm{SGP},\partial_{1}G,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,5}(v)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A_{6,\mathrm{SGP},\partial_{1}G,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{18v^{5}-37v^{4}-120v^{3}+805v^{2}-1140v+480}{60\,v\,(1-v)^{4}}

(154)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt+\frac{w\,(26v^{5}-25v^{4}-120v^{3}+375v^{2}-630v+300)}{30\,v\,(1-v)^{4}}

D.1.4
Soft-Fermion Pole Contribution

The soft-fermion pole contribution is the final part of the
qg\to q
-channel and below we present the explicit expressions for the partonic factors found in Eq. (
67
). We separate the SFP partonic factors by means of their color factors:

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SFP},F}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w,\chi_{m})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N_{c}\,\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SFP},F,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w,\chi_{m})+C_{F}\,\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SFP},F,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w,\chi_{m})\,,

(156)

with the
N_{c}
part given by,

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SFP},F,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w,\chi_{m})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A_{1,\mathrm{SFP},F,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)\,\ln(\chi_{m})+A_{2,\mathrm{SFP},F,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)\,\ln(1-w)

(157)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt+A_{3,\mathrm{SFP},F,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)\,\ln(1-v\,w)+A_{4,\mathrm{SFP},F,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)\,\ln(1-v)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt+A_{5,\mathrm{SFP},F,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)\,\ln(1-v+v\,w)+A_{6,\mathrm{SFP},F,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)\,\ln(w)\,

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt+A_{7,\mathrm{SFP},F,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)\,,

with seven coefficient functions of the following explicit form:

\displaystyle A_{1,\mathrm{SFP},F,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{v^{3}w^{3}}{(1-v)^{4}}-\frac{v^{2}w^{2}}{(1-v)^{3}}-\frac{v\left(v^{2}-4v+5\right)w}{2(1-v)^{4}}

\displaystyle-\frac{v^{2}-4v+2}{(1-v)^{4}}+\frac{3-2v}{(1-v)^{3}(1-v\,w)}-\frac{1}{2(1-v)^{2}(1-v\,w)^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle A_{2,\mathrm{SFP},F,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{2v^{3}w^{3}}{(1-v)^{4}}+\frac{v^{2}(3v-1)w^{2}}{(1-v)^{4}}-\frac{v\left(3v^{2}-4v+5\right)w}{2\,(1-v)^{4}}

\displaystyle-\frac{v^{2}-4v+2}{(1-v)^{4}}+\frac{3-2v}{(1-v)^{3}(1-v\,w)}-\frac{1}{2(1-v)^{2}(1-v\,w)^{2}},

\displaystyle A_{3,\mathrm{SFP},F,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{vw\left(v^{3}(w-1)w^{2}+v^{2}(w-1)w+v-1\right)}{(1-v)^{3}\,(1-v\,w)^{2}},

\displaystyle A_{4,\mathrm{SFP},F,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2v-3}{(1-v)^{2}\,v^{2}\,w}-\frac{2v^{3}-10v^{2}+16v-9}{(1-v)^{3}\,v^{2}}-\frac{w\,(v^{4}-5v^{3}-v^{2}+5v-4)}{2\,(1-v)^{3}\,v^{2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{2(3v-5)w^{2}}{(1-v)^{2}\,v^{2}}-\frac{4v^{2}w-3v(2w+1)+5}{2\,(1-v)^{3}\,(1-v\,w)^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle A_{5,\mathrm{SFP},F,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{vw(2vw-v-1)}{2\,(1-v)^{3}}\,,

\displaystyle A_{6,\mathrm{SFP},F,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2v^{2}-4v+3}{(1-v)^{4}}+\frac{(2-v)vw}{(1-v)^{3}}-\frac{v^{2}(2v+1)w^{2}}{(1-v)^{4}}-\frac{4v^{2}w-3v(2w+1)+5}{2\,(1-v)^{3}\,(1-v\,w)^{2}},

\displaystyle A_{7,\mathrm{SFP},F,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{v^{4}-2v^{3}+24v^{2}-35v+18}{6\,(1-v)^{4}\,v\,w}+\frac{10v^{4}-23v^{3}+126v^{2}-203v+108}{12\,(1-v)^{4}\,v}

(158)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt-\frac{\left(5v^{4}-5v^{3}-29v^{2}+9v-12\right)w}{6\,(1-v)^{4}\,v}

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt-\frac{\left(33v^{4}-16v^{3}+155v^{2}-238v+120\right)w^{2}}{12\,(1-v)^{4}\,v}+\frac{\left(31v^{3}-6v^{2}-v-8\right)w^{3}}{12\,(1-v)^{4}}

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt-\frac{4v^{2}w-v(7w+2)+5}{2\,(1-v)^{3}\,(1-v\,w)^{2}}+\frac{1}{2\,(1-v)\,(1-v+v\,w)}\,.

The
C_{F}
part has a similar form,

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SFP},F,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w,\chi_{m})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A_{1,\mathrm{SFP},F,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)\,\ln(\chi_{m})+A_{2,\mathrm{SFP},F,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)\,\ln(1-w)

(159)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt+A_{3,\mathrm{SFP},F,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)\,\ln(1-v\,w)+A_{4,\mathrm{SFP},F,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)\,\ln(1-v)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt+A_{5,\mathrm{SFP},F,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)\,\ln(1-v+v\,w)+A_{6,\mathrm{SFP},F,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)\,\ln(w)\,

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt+A_{7,\mathrm{SFP},F,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)\,,

as well with seven coefficient functions of the following explicit form:

\displaystyle A_{1,\mathrm{SFP},F,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{5-3v}{(1-v)^{3}}+\frac{w\,v\,\left(v^{2}-6v+3\right)}{(1-v)^{4}}+\frac{2\,w^{2}\,v^{2}\,(2-v)}{(1-v)^{4}}

\displaystyle+\frac{2\,w^{3}\,v^{3}}{(1-v)^{4}}-\frac{4v^{2}w-3v(2w+1)+5}{(1-v)^{3}\,(1-v\,w)^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle A_{2,\mathrm{SFP},F,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{5-3v}{(1-v)^{3}}+\frac{3\,w\,v}{(1-v)^{2}}+\frac{2\,w^{2}\,v^{2}\,(2-3v)}{(1-v)^{4}}+\frac{4\,w^{3}\,v^{3}}{(1-v)^{4}}

\displaystyle-\frac{4v^{2}w-3v(2w+1)+5}{(1-v)^{3}\,(1-v\,w)^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle A_{3,\mathrm{SFP},F,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2vw\left(v^{3}(w-1)w^{2}+v^{2}(w-1)w+v-1\right)}{(1-v)^{3}\,(1-v\,w)^{2}},\,

\displaystyle A_{4,\mathrm{SFP},F,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{6-4v}{(1-v)^{2}v^{2}w}+\frac{4v^{3}-20v^{2}+32v-18}{v^{2}\,(1-v)^{3}}

\displaystyle\hskip-56.9055pt+\frac{w\,\left(v^{4}-5v^{3}-v^{2}+5v-4\right)}{v^{2}\,(1-v)^{3}}-\frac{4\,w^{2}\,(3v-5)}{v^{2}\,(1-v)^{2}}+\frac{4v^{2}w-v(6w+3)+5}{(1-v)^{3}\,(1-v\,w)^{2}},

\displaystyle A_{5,\mathrm{SFP},F,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{v\,w\,(v(1-2w)+1)}{(1-v)^{3}},

\displaystyle A_{6,\mathrm{SFP},F,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{5-3v}{(1-v)^{3}}-\frac{2\,w\,(2-v)v}{(1-v)^{3}}+\frac{2\,w^{2}\,v^{2}(4v-3)}{(1-v)^{4}}-\frac{8\,w^{3}\,v^{3}}{(1-v)^{4}}

\displaystyle+\frac{4v^{2}w-v(6w+3)+5}{(1-v)^{3}\,(1-v\,w)^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle A_{7,\mathrm{SFP},F,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{9v^{5}-22v^{4}-7v^{3}+240v^{2}-390v+180}{30\,v\,(1-v)^{4}\,w}

(160)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt-\frac{75v^{5}-160v^{4}-151v^{3}+1380v^{2}-2220v+1080}{60\,v\,(1-v)^{4}}

\displaystyle+\frac{w\,\left(12v^{5}-14v^{4}+55v^{3}-90v^{2}+105v-60\right)}{15\,v\,(1-v)^{4}}

\displaystyle+\frac{w^{2}\,\left(128v^{5}-65v^{4}-115v^{3}+705v^{2}-1260v+600\right)}{30\,v\,(1-v)^{4}}

\displaystyle-\frac{w^{3}\,v\left(52v^{3}-3v^{2}+6v+1\right)}{6\,(1-v)^{4}}+\frac{5\,w^{4}\,v^{4}}{(1-v)^{4}}

\displaystyle+\frac{4v^{2}w-v(7w+2)+5}{(1-v)^{3}\,(1-v\,w)^{2}}-\frac{1}{(1-v)\,(1-v+v\,w)}\,.

The partonic cross section for the corresponding SFP derivative term reads,

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SFP},\partial_{2}F}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w,\chi_{m})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N_{c}\,\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SFP},\partial_{2}F,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w,\chi_{m})+C_{F}\,\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SFP},\partial_{2}F,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w,\chi_{m})\,,

(161)

with the
N_{c}
part given by,

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SFP},\partial_{2}F,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)

\displaystyle A_{1,\mathrm{SFP},\partial_{2}F,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)\,\ln(1-v)+A_{2,\mathrm{SFP},\partial_{2}F,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)\,\ln(w)\hskip 0.0pt+A_{3,\mathrm{SFP},\partial_{2}F,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)\,,

(162)

with three coefficient functions of the following explicit form:

\displaystyle A_{1,\mathrm{SFP},\partial_{2}F,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{v\left(-6w^{2}+8w-2\right)+10w^{2}-12w+3}{2\,w\,v^{2}\,(1-v)^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle A_{2,\mathrm{SFP},\partial_{2}F,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{w\,v^{2}\,(1+v\,w)}{(1-v)^{4}}\,,

\displaystyle A_{3,\mathrm{SFP},\partial_{2}F,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{v^{4}-2v^{3}+24v^{2}-35v+18}{12\,w\,v\,(1-v)^{4}}-\frac{7v^{4}-16v^{3}+96v^{2}-144v+72}{12\,v\,(1-v)^{4}}

(163)

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt+\frac{\left(11v^{4}-9v^{3}+38v^{2}-63v+30\right)w}{6\,v\,(1-v)^{4}}-\frac{\left(31v^{3}-6v^{2}-v-8\right)\,w^{2}}{24\,(1-v)^{4}}.

The
C_{F}
part reads

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SFP},\partial_{2}F,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A_{1,\mathrm{SFP},\partial_{2}F,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)\,\ln(1-v)+A_{2,\mathrm{SFP},\partial_{2}F,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)\,\ln(w)\hskip 0.0pt+A_{3,\mathrm{SFP},\partial_{2}F,NC_{F}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)\,,

(164)

with three coefficient functions of the following explicit form:

\displaystyle A_{1,\mathrm{SFP},\partial_{2}F,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{v\left(6w^{2}-8w+2\right)-10w^{2}+12w-3}{w\,v^{2}\,(1-v)^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle A_{2,\mathrm{SFP},\partial_{2}F,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2v^{3}w^{2}}{(1-v)^{4}}\,,

\displaystyle A_{3,\mathrm{SFP},\partial_{2}F,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,1}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{-9v^{5}+22v^{4}+7v^{3}-240v^{2}+390v-180}{60\,w\,v\,(1-v)^{4}}

(165)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt+\frac{27v^{5}-61v^{4}-15v^{3}+480v^{2}-780v+360}{30\,v\,(1-v)^{4}}

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt-\frac{w\,\left(64v^{5}-150v^{4}+15v^{3}+360v^{2}-630v+300\right)}{30\,v\,(1-v)^{4}}

\displaystyle+\frac{w^{2}\,v\,\left(28v^{3}-39v^{2}+6v+1\right)}{12\,(1-v)^{4}}-\frac{v^{4}w^{3}}{(1-v)^{4}}.

We now turn to the SFP contributions by the quark-gluon-quark correlation function
G
. The corresponding partonic factor reads,

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SFP},G}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w,\chi_{m})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle N_{c}\,\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SFP},G,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w,\chi_{m})+C_{F}\,\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SFP},G,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w,\chi_{m})\,,

(166)

with the
N_{c}
part given by,

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SFP},G,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w,\chi_{m})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A_{1,\mathrm{SFP},G,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)\,\ln(\chi_{m})+A_{2,\mathrm{SFP},G,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)\,\ln(1-w)

(167)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt+A_{3,\mathrm{SFP},G,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)\,\ln(1-v\,w)+A_{4,\mathrm{SFP},G,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)\,\ln(1-v)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt+A_{5,\mathrm{SFP},G,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)\,\ln(1-v+v\,w)+A_{6,\mathrm{SFP},G,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)\,\ln(w)\,

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt+A_{7,\mathrm{SFP},G,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)\,,

with seven coefficient functions of the following explicit form:

\displaystyle A_{1,\mathrm{SFP},G,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{v^{3}w^{3}}{(1-v)^{4}}-\frac{v^{2}w^{2}}{(1-v)^{3}}-\frac{v\left(v^{2}-4v+5\right)w}{2(1-v)^{4}}-\frac{v^{2}-4v+2}{(1-v)^{4}}

\displaystyle+\frac{4v^{2}w-v(6w+3)+5}{2\,(1-v)^{3}\,(1-v\,w)^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle A_{2,\mathrm{SFP},G,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{v^{2}(1+v)w^{2}}{(1-v)^{4}}-\frac{v^{2}-4v+2}{(1-v)^{4}}-\frac{v(5+v)w}{2\,(1-v)^{3}}

\displaystyle+\frac{4v^{2}w-v(6w+3)+5}{2\,(1-v)^{3}\,(1-v\,w)^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle A_{3,\mathrm{SFP},G,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{v^{2}w^{2}}{(1-v)^{3}}-\frac{(3-v)\,v\,w}{(1-v)^{3}}+\frac{3v-5}{(1-v)^{3}}+\frac{4v^{2}w-v(6w+3)+5}{(1-v)^{3}\,(1-v\,w)^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle A_{4,\mathrm{SFP},G,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2v-3}{v^{2}\,(1-v)^{2}\,w}-\frac{2v^{3}-10v^{2}+16v-9}{v^{2}\,(1-v)^{3}}+\frac{\left(3v^{3}-v+4\right)w}{2\,v^{2}\,(1-v)^{2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{2(3v-5)w^{2}}{v^{2}\,(1-v)^{2}}-\frac{4v^{2}w-3v(2w+1)+5}{2\,(1-v)^{3}\,(1-v\,w)^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle A_{5,\mathrm{SFP},G,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{v^{2}w^{2}}{(1-v)^{3}}+\frac{v(1+v)\,w}{2\,(1-v)^{3}}\,,

\displaystyle A_{6,\mathrm{SFP},G,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2v^{3}w^{3}}{(1-v)^{4}}+\frac{3v^{2}w^{2}}{(1-v)^{4}}+\frac{(2-v)v\,w}{(1-v)^{3}}+\frac{2v^{2}-4v+3}{(1-v)^{4}}

\displaystyle-\frac{4v^{2}w-3v(2w+1)+5}{2\,(1-v)^{3}\,(1-v\,w)^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle A_{7,\mathrm{SFP},G,N_{c}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{v^{4}-2v^{3}-24v^{2}+37v-18}{6\,(1-v)^{4}\,v\,w}-\frac{10v^{4}-35v^{3}-102v^{2}+181v-108}{12(1-v)^{4}v}

(168)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt+\frac{\left(5v^{4}-17v^{3}+23v^{2}-33v+12\right)w}{6(1-v)^{4}v}+\frac{\left(9v^{4}+8v^{3}-149v^{2}+242v-120\right)w^{2}}{12(1-v)^{4}v}

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt-\frac{\left(7v^{3}-6v^{2}-v-8\right)w^{3}}{12(1-v)^{4}}-\frac{4v^{2}w-v(7w+2)+5}{2\,(1-v)^{3}\,(1-v\,w)^{2}}-\frac{1}{2\,(1-v)\,(1-v+v\,w)}\,.

On the other hand, the
C_{F}
part is given by,

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{SFP},G,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w,\chi_{m})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle A_{1,\mathrm{SFP},G,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)\,\ln(\chi_{m})+A_{2,\mathrm{SFP},G,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)\,\ln(1-w)

(169)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt+A_{3,\mathrm{SFP},G,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)\,\ln(1-v\,w)+A_{4,\mathrm{SFP},G,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)\,\ln(1-v)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt+A_{5,\mathrm{SFP},G,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)\,\ln(1-v+v\,w)+A_{6,\mathrm{SFP},G,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)\,\ln(w)\,

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt+A_{7,\mathrm{SFP},G,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)\,,

with seven coefficient functions of the following explicit form:

\displaystyle A_{1,\mathrm{SFP},G,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2v^{3}w^{3}}{(1-v)^{4}}-\frac{2(v-2)v^{2}w^{2}}{(1-v)^{4}}+\frac{v\left(v^{2}-6v+3\right)w}{(1-v)^{4}}+\frac{5-3v}{(1-v)^{3}}-\frac{4v^{2}w-3v(2w+1)+5}{(1-v)^{3}\,(1-v\,w)^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle A_{2,\mathrm{SFP},G,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{2v^{2}(v+2)w^{2}}{(1-v)^{4}}-\frac{v\left(v^{2}+6v-3\right)w}{(1-v)^{4}}+\frac{5-3v}{(1-v)^{3}}-\frac{4v^{2}w-3v(2w+1)+5}{(1-v)^{3}\,(1-v\,w)^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle A_{3,\mathrm{SFP},G,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{2v^{2}w^{2}}{(1-v)^{3}}+\frac{2(3-v)v\,w}{(1-v)^{3}}+\frac{2(5-3v)}{(1-v)^{3}}-\frac{2\left(4v^{2}w-3v(2w+1)+5\right)}{(1-v)^{3}\,(1-v\,w)^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle A_{4,\mathrm{SFP},G,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{6-4v}{(1-v)^{2}v^{2}\,w}+\frac{4v^{3}-20v^{2}+32v-18}{(1-v)^{3}v^{2}}-\frac{\left(3v^{3}-v+4\right)\,w}{(1-v)^{2}v^{2}}

\displaystyle+\frac{4(5-3v)\,w^{2}}{(1-v)^{2}v^{2}}+\frac{4v^{2}w-v(6w+3)+5}{(1-v)^{3}\,(1-v\,w)^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\frac{v(1+v)w}{(1-v)^{3}}+\frac{2v^{2}w^{2}}{(1-v)^{3}}\,,

\displaystyle A_{6,\mathrm{SFP},G,C_{F}}^{qg\to q,5}(v,w)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{4v^{3}w^{3}}{(1-v)^{4}}-\frac{2v^{2}(2v+1)w^{2}}{(1-v)^{4}}+\frac{2(v-2)vw}{(1-v)^{3}}+\frac{3v-5}{(1-v)^{3}}

\displaystyle+\frac{4v^{2}w-v(6w+3)+5}{(1-v)^{3}\,(1-v\,w)^{2}}\,,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{-9v^{5}+22v^{4}-33v^{3}+240v^{2}-390v+180}{30(1-v)^{4}vw}

(170)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt+\frac{75v^{5}-160v^{4}+129v^{3}-1140v^{2}+2100v-1080}{60\,v\,(1-v)^{4}}

\displaystyle-\frac{\left(12v^{5}-14v^{4}-75v^{3}+60v^{2}-105v+60\right)w}{15\,v\,(1-v)^{4}}

\displaystyle-\frac{\left(128v^{5}-185v^{4}+325v^{3}-735v^{2}+1260v-600\right)w^{2}}{30\,v\,(1-v)^{4}}

read explicitly,

\displaystyle\hat{\sigma}_{\mathrm{Int},1}^{qq\to q,1}(v,w,\zeta)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N_{c}}\frac{w}{4(1-\zeta)\zeta(1-v)^{4}}\left[v(w-\zeta)^{2}\left(\zeta(2\zeta-1)\right.\right.

(176)

\displaystyle\hskip 0.0pt\left.+v^{2}\left(\zeta(2\zeta-1)+\left(8\zeta^{2}-4\zeta-2\right)w^{2}+2(3-4\zeta)\zeta w\right)-2v\left(\zeta^{2}-3\zeta w+w\right)\right)
